=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums and a positive integer k. Return the sum of the maximum and minimum elements of all subsequences of nums with at most k elements.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,3], k = 2
Output: 24
Explanation:
The subsequences of nums with at most 2 elements are:



Subsequence 
Minimum
Maximum
Sum


[1]
1
1
2


[2]
2
2
4


[3]
3
3
6


[1, 2]
1
2
3


[1, 3]
1
3
4


[2, 3]
2
3
5


Final Total
 
 
24



The output would be 24.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [5,0,6], k = 1
Output: 22
Explanation: 
For subsequences with exactly 1 element, the minimum and maximum values are the element itself. Therefore, the total is 5 + 5 + 0 + 0 + 6 + 6 = 22.

Example 3:

Input: nums = [1,1,1], k = 2
Output: 12
Explanation:
The subsequences [1, 1] and [1] each appear 3 times. For all of them, the minimum and maximum are both 1. Thus, the total is 12.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10^5
0 <= nums[i] <= 10^9
1 <= k <= min(70, nums.length)

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo:

from typing import List
class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9+7
        nums.sort()
        n = len(nums)
        K = k - 1
        
        # f[m] = sum_{r=0}^{K} C(m,r) mod MOD for m=0..n-1
        f = [0]*n
        f[0] = 1  # since C(0,0)=1, if K>=0
        if K == 0:
            # f[m] = 1 for all m
            for m in range(1,n):
                f[m] = 1
        else:
            # compute combK[m] = C(m,K) for m=0..n-1 maybe
            # Need inv up to n maybe
            inv = [0]*(n+1)
            if n >= 1: inv[1] = 1
            for i in range(2, n+1):
                inv[i] = MOD - (MOD//i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD
            combK = [0]*n  # indices 0..n-1
            if K < n:
                combK[K] = 1
                for m in range(K, n-1):
                    combK[m+1] = combK[m] * (m+1) % MOD * inv[m+1-K] % MOD
            # But if K >= n? k<=n so K<=n-1, okay.
            f[0] = 1
            for m in range(0, n-1):
                f[m+1] = (2*f[m] - combK[m]) % MOD
        ans = 0
        for i, val in enumerate(nums):
            ans = (ans + val % MOD * (f[i] + f[n-1-i])) % MOD
        return ans

Let's write code:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        nums.sort()
        n = len(nums)
        K = k - 1

        # f[m] = sum_{r=0}^{K} C(m, r) mod MOD, for m=0..n-1
        f = [0] * n
        f[0] = 1

        if K == 0:
            for i in range(1, n):
                f[i] = 1
        else:
            # modular inverses up to n
            inv = [0] * (n + 1)
            inv[1] = 1
            for i in range(2, n + 1):
                inv[i] = MOD - (MOD // i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD

            # combK[m] = C(m, K) mod MOD, for m=0..n-1
            combK = [0] * n
            if K < n:
                combK[K] = 1
                for m in range(K, n - 1):
                    combK[m + 1] = combK[m] * (m + 1) % MOD * inv[m + 1 - K] % MOD

            for m in range(n - 1):
                f[m + 1] = (2 * f[m] - combK[m]) % MOD

        ans = 0
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            ans = (ans + (x % MOD) * (f[i] + f[n - 1 - i])) % MOD
        return ans

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with code style: The final code should be in a code block, no extra explanation. We'll output:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        nums.sort()
        n = len(nums)
        K = k - 1

        f = [0] * n
        f[0] = 1

        if K == 0:
            for i in range(1, n):
                f[i] = 1
        else:
            inv = [0] * (n + 1)
            inv[1] = 1
            for i in range(2, n + 1):
                inv[i] = MOD - (MOD // i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD

            combK = [0] * n
            combK[K] = 1
            for m in range(K, n - 1):
                combK[m + 1] = combK[m] * (m + 1) % MOD * inv[m + 1 - K] % MOD

            for m in range(n - 1):
                f[m + 1] = (2 * f[m] - combK[m]) % MOD

        ans = 0
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            ans = (ans + (x % MOD) * (f[i] + f[n - 1 - i])) % MOD
        return ans
```

Now, maybe there is a hidden issue with "subsequence" if original array order and sorted order mapping changes the count of subsequences where a particular original element is min/max? Let's think more rigorously: Sorting defines a bijection π from original indices to sorted indices. For any subset S of original indices, let T=π(S). The multiset of values is same, so min/max values same. The count of subsets T where sorted position j is the smallest selected index equals the number of original subsets S where π^{-1}(j) is the "smallest selected sorted index", i.e., among selected original elements, its value is minimum (and if ties, it is the earliest in sorted order among selected equal values). But does this correspond to "original element is min value" not necessarily? We are summing values, not identities. For each subset, exactly one sorted position is smallest selected, and its value is the min value. Summing over sorted positions of value * count_min_j gives sum of min values over all subsets. This is valid regardless of original identities. We don't need to count original element as min in original order; we count sorted position as min in sorted subset. Since mapping is bijective, sum over sorted subsets equals sum over original subsets. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "subsequence" if original order and sorted order mapping changes the set of subsequences? It maps subsets, and every subset corresponds to a subsequence. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "Return the sum ... modulo 10^9+7" and if answer can
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        nums.sort()
        n = len(nums)
        K = k - 1

        # f[m] = sum_{r=0}^{K} C(m, r) mod MOD, for m = 0..n-1
        f = [0] * n
        f[0] = 1

        if K == 0:
            for i in range(1, n):
                f[i] = 1
        else:
            # Modular inverses up to n
            inv = [0] * (n + 1)
            inv[1] = 1
            for i in range(2, n + 1):
                inv[i] = MOD - (MOD // i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD

            # combK[m] = C(m, K) mod MOD
            combK = [0] * n
            combK[K] = 1
            for m in range(K, n - 1):
                combK[m + 1] = combK[m] * (m + 1) % MOD * inv[m + 1 - K] % MOD

            # S(m+1) = 2*S(m) - C(m, K)
            for m in range(n - 1):
                f[m + 1] = (2 * f[m] - combK[m]) % MOD

        ans = 0
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            ans = (ans + (x % MOD) * (f[i] + f[n - 1 - i])) % MOD

        return ans
```
